Abstract: A defense that disrupts hidden messages may still provide little protection if its detector rarely triggers an intervention. We present StegoGuard-LM, a controlled framework for evaluating defenses against covert messages in large language model outputs. We examine token-rank and synonym channels, with Unicode concealment as a structural control, and track messages through detection, intervention, and recovery. Deterministic rewriting prevented full recovery of 103 of 104 synonym-encoded messages when applied unconditionally, but only one when intervention depended on a detector set to limit false alarms. In a four-round replay on a 48-response subset, an attacker selecting from a fixed catalog recovered more bits by switching channels, although paired gains were not statistically significant after correction for multiple comparisons. Randomized rewriting showed no sustained advantage in this pilot. A more sensitive detector improved protection but modified about one-third of clean responses. These findings show how uneven detection can undermine effective rewriting and why protection must be evaluated alongside attacker adaptation and intervention on legitimate text.

</details>

Abstract: Phishing remains one of the most pervasive and damaging cyber threats, exploiting both technical flaws and human behavior to compromise sensitive data. We present a privacy-preserving, real-time defense that analyzes full webpage source code on mobile platforms. Our system combines a lightweight Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) for end-to-end feature extraction with a Federated Learning (FL) framework that updates models collaboratively on user devices without transferring raw data-supporting regulatory compliance and personalization. Evaluated on a large and diverse dataset, the model attains 86.02\% accuracy, 90.35\% precision, 80.64\% recall, and 92.78\% ROC-AUC. While some centralized, classical approaches may report higher accuracy, our design emphasizes a practical balance among accuracy, user privacy, and real-time performance on resource-constrained devices. We further demonstrate an end-to-end integration within the open-source Lightning Android browser, achieving real-time detection with low computational overhead. This work shows that privacy-first, AI-driven phishing detection is both feasible and scalable across mobile and IoT settings, delivering robust protection while keeping user data on-device.

</details>

Abstract: A software-defined vehicle depends on products that sit under different EU regimes. The type-approved vehicle and the systems, components and separate technical units made for it fall under Regulation (EU) 2019/2144, which makes UN R155 mandatory; Regulation (EU) 2018/858 does so for R156; and ISO/SAE 21434 supports the management system R155 requires. Chargers, companion apps, aftermarket devices and many backends may fall under the Cyber Resilience Act (CRA) instead, whose reporting duty in Article 14 has applied since 11 September 2026. Neither side defines how a vulnerability found on one reaches the other's risk process. We contribute a four-question classification procedure, a five-regime traceability matrix, a reference architecture with four handoff paths and three boundary findings. On 75 classified products from a frozen public-source corpus, the procedure yields 47 Periphery, 11 Core, 1 Other and 16 Boundary products, three of them outside both the CRA and compulsory R155. Because UN R10 lets an electronic sub-assembly be approved as a component, the texts leave a second reading open, and we report both counts. Under it, 22 aftermarket devices become components, Periphery falls to 25 and 25 products sit outside both regimes. Seven retrospective walkthroughs suggest that, for third-party products, vulnerability information stops in the vehicle manufacturer's own inventory, triggers or exploitation assessment, and that no provision obliges a product maker to tell a vehicle manufacturer: the only defined two-way route is a contract. No reviewed source establishes a case that reached an Article 14 clock.

</details>

Abstract: Conventional Machine Learning (ML)-based Intrusion Detection Systems (IDSs) require manual design, optimize only detection performance, and neglect inference latency and model size, which are critical for resource-constrained Internet of Things (IoT) and Electric Vehicle Charging System (EVCS) deployments. Automated ML (AutoML) reduces manual effort but explores broad search spaces inefficiently. Meanwhile, Multi-Objective Optimization (MOO) methods lack guided search initialization, leading to long search times. This paper proposes a Large Language Model-Assisted Multi-Objective Automated ML (LLM-MOO-AutoML) framework for efficient intrusion detection. A local Llama 3.1 8B model analyzes a compact dataset profile and generates a bounded, validated policy for automated data balancing (AutoDP) and feature engineering (AutoFE). This policy narrows the search space for Non-dominated Sorting Genetic Algorithm III (NSGA-III), which jointly optimizes the LightGBM feature-selection threshold and key hyperparameters across three objectives: maximizing weighted F1-score, minimizing 99th-percentile (P99) inference latency ratio, and minimizing model size ratio. Experiments on CICIDS2017, IoTID20, and CICEVSE2024 demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves the highest weighted F1-scores on CICIDS2017 (99.788%), IoTID20 (99.778%), and CICEVSE2024 (99.989%), with feature sets reduced to as few as 10 features, P99 latency as low as 0.00038 ms, and model size as small as 0.1073 MB. The LLM-guided configuration reduced NSGA-III search time relative to unconstrained multi-objective search on all datasets at the largest trial budget and in most comparisons at smaller budgets, indicating that dataset-aware search-space reduction supports efficient, deployment-aware intrusion detection.

</details>

Abstract: This paper investigates the cyber-economic impact and pre-clearing detectability of false data injection attacks (FDIAs) in peer-to-peer (P2P) energy trading among interconnected microgrids. In the proposed cyber-economic framework, load and photovoltaic profiles drive a sequential 15-minute market with deterministic double-sided proportional (pro-rata) allocation and supply-to-demand-ratio (SDR) pricing, while true physical injections are separated from communicated market quantities. The framework is evaluated through a multi-microgrid case study over 69,904 market-aligned states, for which all AC power-flow solutions converged and voltages remained within 0.98999-1.04369 per unit. Healthy local exchange reached 1,159.66 MWh and generated USD 92,772.62 of community benefit. Bias, scaling, and ramp attacks modify only one microgrid's communicated quantity, altering the settlement price, P2P allocation, scheduled residual-grid exchange, participant financial outcomes, and benefit fairness, while true feeder injections remain unchanged. Role-specific random forest, statistical threshold, and score-fusion detectors are compared using only information available before clearing. Pooled sample-level detection rates range from 7.82% to 12.14%, with false-alarm rates from 4.69% to 7.70% and role dependence. In the investigated case study, measurable downstream economic distortion and low pre-clearing detectability coexist under bounded quantity manipulation.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) property attestation lets a model provider demonstrate properties of their models and datasets to verifiers, and trusted execution environments (TEEs) have emerged as its most practical mechanism. However, existing TEE-based systems attest only a static snapshot of the TEE's initial configuration, leaving run-time behavior unauditable. Proof of execution (PoX) architectures close this gap by requiring the prover's root of trust to also demonstrate that (1) the attested operation actually executed and (2) the claimed outputs were affected only by that operation. Existing PoX designs, though, target microcontrollers and rely on hardware features unavailable in server-grade TEEs, so they cannot be applied directly to ML workloads.
  We present EVOLVE, a software-based PoX framework for server-grade TEEs. EVOLVE mediates an operation's writes and control flow to track whether a requested ML operation executes without violation. It binds the execution status to measurements of the operation's inputs and outputs, enabling verifiers to assess run-time behavior through ML property attestation. We implement EVOLVE on Intel TDX as a Rust module within its virtual firmware, with policy checks built on primitives formally verified in F*. Our evaluation shows that EVOLVE is versatile across diverse ML applications on unmodified hardware, provides formal guarantees for its policy checks. EVOLVE incurs per-request run-time overhead on average ranging from 1.07$\times$ (for decision trees) to 2.13$\times$ (for multi-layer perceptrons).

</details>

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) signals contain sensitive, subject-specific information, making models trained on them potential targets for membership inference attacks (MIAs). Although MIAs have been studied extensively in other domains, their applications to EEG models are relatively scarce, and the factors driving the vulnerability of EEG models to MIAs are not adequately understood. In this paper, we present a systematic empirical analysis of MIAs on EEG models using three datasets, seven model architectures, four attack methods, and two metrics. Results show that poor model generalization (large train-test loss and accuracy gap) is the strongest indicator of MIA vulnerability. Longer training and larger models also increase vulnerability. Moreover, no particular scalp region, frequency band, inter-channel correlation pattern, or temporal versus spectral representation of EEG data consistently explains MIA vulnerability across datasets. Finally, subject identification and leave-one-subject-out experiments indicate that models relying more heavily on subject-specific representations are more vulnerable to MIAs. Overall, our findings identify the key drivers of membership leakage in EEG models and can provide guidance for developing MIA-resilient EEG systems.

</details>

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) predict the label of a query row conditioned on a context of labelled rows, without training on them. Drawn from operational data, the context becomes an attack surface. We study targeted in-context poisoning in its simplest form: an attacker with write access to the context's source places exact copies of a target row there with the wrong label. On 30 OpenML datasets for binary classification, one such poison row flips on average 53% (TabICL) and 65% (TabPFN) of the predictions correct without attack, and three poison rows flip almost all. The same copy with the true label flips almost none, and performance on the remaining rows hardly changes. Predictions flip by two routes: in TabICL, the target row's embedding rotates towards the mean of the wrong class; in TabPFN, the poison row pulls genuine rows of the wrong class along. The TFMs thus infer soundly, but from forged evidence. Predictions of trained models flip as well: almost always for a random forest, for CatBoost as often as for the TFMs, hardly for a logistic regression. New with TFMs is not the kind of vulnerability but the timing: the data take effect at the moment of prediction, not through training, and a poison row in a live context can be deleted afterwards. Three established requirements must therefore cover the context: access control for its source, traceability per prediction, which can also deter, and validation at prediction time. A detection mechanism shows how such validation can work.

</details>

Abstract: Distributed backdoor attacks split a trigger among several federated learning clients, so that the backdoor appears only in the aggregated model. Since DBA, a line of work has reported that such attacks are stronger, more persistent, and stealthier than a single attacker with the full trigger. We systematize this literature and test this belief. We code nineteen primary studies and fifteen papers that evaluate against DBA. The reported advantage is rarely measured against a complete trigger delivered by the same attackers, and none of the fifteen evaluations tests whether individual pieces remain effective. We then run 875 paired federated runs that separate adding attackers from splitting the trigger. In our image experiments, adding attackers raised attack success in every seed of all 30 cells where we measured it, by 7.5 to 83.4 percentage points on average. Splitting the trigger among the same attackers had a smaller effect, and its sign depended on the piece layout, the allocation, and the attackers' local training. With DBA's compact layout, splitting lowered attack success on CIFAR-10. A larger penalty on small CNNs vanished once the attackers trained longer, and an FCBA-style allocation gained a few points. Widely separated pieces helped on CIFAR-10, its grayscale version, and GTSRB. No model required all pieces. Neural Cleanse suppressed single pieces together with the full trigger, but split backdoors with separated pieces survived it better than complete ones. We derive reporting requirements for attack and defense evaluations.

</details>

Abstract: Open-weight language models are vulnerable to trigger-conditioned behaviors introduced through sparse post-training weight modifications, creating supply-chain risks beyond conventional provenance checks. We present Neural Sentinel, a cross-architecture study of gradient-guided backdoor insertion and model forensics spanning 18 checkpoints across 14 architecture families (124M-31.3B parameters), including state-space and recurrent models.
  On Phi-2, modifying approximately 0.0002% of parameters induced an exact short-marker output after 300 steps (332 seconds), with mean next-token KL divergence of $1.5 \times 10^{-5}$ and 100% top-1 agreement over evaluated positions of eight fixed reference prompts. Separate experiments produced structured tool-call-formatted outputs without establishing downstream execution. We identify three procedure-dependent response regimes -- Gradient-Permeable, Scale-Threshold, and Gradient-Fragmented -- and evaluate PPO-based weight-space exploration, which showed no statistically significant advantage over random search.
  For defense, Neural Sentinel combines weight-difference analysis, trigger discovery, activation probing, and contextual output inspection. In a 22-artifact development cohort, its reference-optional scanner classified 4/5 backdoored artifacts as BACKDOORED; the remaining positive and all 17 clean artifacts were SUSPICIOUS. These findings establish the feasibility of sparse conditional-output insertion and evidence-producing forensic triage, while identifying limitations in behavioral-drift assessment and clean-model admission.

</details>

Abstract: Witness encryption encrypts a message to an NP statement so that whoever holds a witness can decrypt. Over a decade after its introduction, known constructions for general NP statements are far from practical or rest on heuristic or broken assumptions. We propose Praxis, a practical scheme for NP, and prove it extractable in the bilinear generic group and random oracle models under Module-SIS. Our instantiation encrypts to a Groth16 verifier on BN254, set up honestly, which serves every NP relation at the same cost: at $100$-bit security, its ciphertext is $7.88$ GiB, encryption takes $2^{34.2}$ base-field multiplications, and decryption with a Groth16 proof $2^{33.0}$ on average. We give Lean 4 proofs of correctness and extraction at this instantiation's parameters.
  Praxis starts from the rank-gap template of the recent feasibility result [Hair and Sahai, arXiv 2026]. Where they raise the gap to keep decryption polynomial, we fix it, so decryption ends with a search that is exponential but concretely fast: $O(2^{λ/4} N^{1/4})$ expected operations for a statement in $N$ Boolean unknowns at $λ$-bit security. A quadratic arithmetic program makes our hidden matrix low rank, so where their ciphertext encodes a basis of a matrix space, ours holds factors of width two, which a pairing multiplies. Weights and identities from an extension field tie copies of this matrix, so an adversary telling encryptions apart yields a witness or a Module-SIS solution. For the same Groth16 statement and $100$-bit security level, Praxis reduces the ciphertext size and the cost of encryption of either of their constructions by over ten orders of magnitude, and that of decryption by over six.

</details>

Abstract: Developing deep learning models requires substantial computational and data resources, making them core intellectual assets for enterprises. As Deep Learning as a Service (DLaaS) increasingly extends to edge computing environments, models deployed on third-party devices face growing threats from reverse engineering. Existing model protection methods often depend on specialized hardware or incur substantial computational overhead, while lacking effective mechanisms for controlling model usage. There is therefore a need for lightweight solutions that combine model protection with controllable authorization. To address this challenge, we propose KeyObf, a model obfuscation and controllable authorization scheme for DLaaS. KeyObf efficiently protects model parameters and architecture through model obfuscation while binding parameter recovery to user keys, ensuring that only authorized users can perform inference correctly. By integrating a key-based mechanism into the obfuscation framework, KeyObf unifies model protection and controllable authorization. Experimental results demonstrate that KeyObf preserves the inference accuracy of authorized users, rejects inference requests when credential authentication fails, and increases the difficulty of recovering model information under the evaluated reverse-engineering conditions. These results demonstrate the feasibility of a lightweight approach to model protection and controllable authorization in third-party deployment scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: In 2001 we published a paper on an Anti OS Detection (AoD) system: a modified FreeBSD 4.3 kernel that makes a host answer the probes of an OS fingerprinting tool as if it ran another operating system. This report revisits that work on FreeBSD 15.1. We do not claim a new method. We report three things. First, the same goal is now reachable from a loadable module of about 800 lines that uses the packet filter hook interface and sets one kernel variable, without changing the kernel source. Second, the module covers both active and passive fingerprinting: Nmap 7.98 reports an exact match for Linux and for Windows 10/11, and p0f 3 reports the same systems from the connections the host opens. Third, an exact match in Nmap was not enough: a passive tool, plain FIN scans and a comparison with real hosts each exposed a mismatch that the previous step could not see. A real Windows 11 host and the final Windows personality get the same verdict from Nmap and the same signatures from p0f and JA4T; a real Linux 7.0 host is not an exact match in Nmap at all, while the personality built from Nmap's database is. We describe those mismatches, how we removed them, what the module costs, and what it still cannot hide.

</details>

Abstract: Tor end-to-end correlation attacks can link a client to its destination by matching traffic observed at both ends of a Tor circuit. However, their practical threat remains unclear for two reasons: existing correlation methods rely on O(N^2) pairwise processing, and prior works do not reflect the flow counts and base rates seen by realistic adversaries. We present InstantCoFFEA, which replaces exhaustive pairwise search with approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) retrieval using CAGRA and introduces ranked voting. At N = 200k, InstantCoFFEA is 14.56x faster than DeepCoFFEA and improves PR-AUC from 0.94 to 0.96. We also use TorPS to estimate the flow counts and matching-pair base rates observed by relay-level and network-level adversaries. Our simulations show that realistic attacks involve substantially more candidate flows and much lower base rates than those used in prior evaluations. Under a low-base-rate setting, PR-AUC drops for all methods, but InstantCoFFEA achieves 0.53 compared with 0.42 for DeepCoFFEA. These results show that evaluating correlation attacks requires considering both computational scale and the sparsity of true matching pairs.

</details>

Abstract: QR-UOV is a Quotient Ring Unbalanced Oil and Vinegar (UOV) multivariate quadratic (MQ) signature scheme that has progressed to round-3 of the post-quantum cryptography (PQC) additional signatures call by the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). Until now, for architectural efficiency and performance trade-offs, QR-UOV has been evaluated only on software platforms and has never been considered on hardware, such as field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs). To address this, we are the first to provide a complete QR-UOV hardware implementation through a flexible and unified QUISA coprocessor design, which supports instruction-driven execution for key generation, signing, and verification across all three NIST security levels (SL-I, SL-III, SL-V). The proposed architecture features extensive throughput optimizations by parallelizing key QR-UOV module operations, such as the pseudorandom byte generator, hashing, and rejection sampling. To minimize hardware resources, we propose a modular arithmetic unit with finite-field reduction based on the shift-add folding method. The design introduces a linear system solver for QUISA based on LU decomposition with partial pivoting and a sample solution module, operating over the prime field Fq (q = 127). We comprehensively evaluate QUISA on an Artix-7 FPGA at 80 MHz up to the post-place-and-route stage, utilizing 11,144 slices and 35,248 look-up tables. For higher SL-V security levels, it completes key generation, signing, and verification in 30.4M, 3.8M, and 2.8M clock cycles, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: AI coding agents now triage issues, review pull requests and pick up tasks inside GitHub repositories, and to do so they read text that anyone with a GitHub account can write. This makes GitHub a prime surface for indirect prompt injection, in which instructions embedded in untrusted content steer a model that later processes it. Prior work has demonstrated such attacks against agents on GitHub in controlled settings, but how often they occur in practice, who deploys them, and for what purpose remain unknown.
  We present the first large-scale empirical study of AI-directed hidden text on GitHub. We analyzed roughly 145 million issues, pull requests, comments and README files, detected text that GitHub does not render to a human reader but that an agent receives in full, and filtered it with a two-stage LLM classification, a template provenance check and an exhaustive manual review. We identify 2,281 validated findings in issues, pull requests and comments (16 per million items), and AI-directed hidden text in the READMEs of 107 repositories.
  We find a heterogeneous ecosystem of four uses: attacks on other parties' agents, defensive text that restricts agents, content override that steers what they write, and first-party orchestration, in which projects coordinate their own agents through hidden markers. Attacks are rare (about one in 3.3 million items) but aimed at maintainers' own triage, review and CI agents. Orchestration accounts for two thirds of the findings in issues, pull requests and comments, grew fivefold in four months, and forms a control channel that anyone who can comment can imitate. Replayed offline against five models we tested, the attacks succeed against one small, fast model in 19% of cases, and against none once HTML comments are stripped. We release our detection tool and every finding.

</details>

Abstract: The large value $\varepsilon=19.61$, reported as an approximate differential privacy (ADP) parameter for the 2020 U.S. Census, has prompted doubts about the adequacy of Census privacy protection. It was obtained by converting component zero-concentrated differential privacy (zCDP) guarantees to ADP at $δ=10^{-10}$. A single ADP pair can substantially loosen the original upper bound on the power (true positive rate) of a distinguishing test. At significance level $α$, we express the original bound as the smallest pure differential privacy (DP) parameter giving the same power bound. We call this the $α$-operationally equivalent $\varepsilon$. The full curve represents the original bound exactly on $(0,1)$, even when the bound is nonconcave. For maximum-power curves and bounds derived from trade-off functions, we characterize the extrema of operationally equivalent $\varepsilon$ over an interval of significance levels. When finite, they give the parameters of the pure DP bounds that enclose the original power bound on that interval. The mapping preserves the ordering of power bounds at each significance level. For Laplace and Gaussian mechanisms with equal noise variance, conversion to single ADP pairs or Gaussian DP envelopes can reverse this ordering. For the P.L. 94-171 redistricting data, we reproduce the reported $\varepsilon=19.61$ as the sum of rounded component ADP values. The published zCDP guarantee yields operationally equivalent $\varepsilon$ values of approximately 4.25, 2.94, and 3.18 at significance levels of 1%, 5%, and 10%, respectively. Converting the same guarantee to a single ADP pair at $δ=10^{-10}$ gives power bounds near 1 at all three levels. The pure DP scale thus clarifies constraints on distinguishing attacks that are difficult to discern from the reported $\varepsilon=19.61$.

</details>

Abstract: Large audio-language models (LALMs) have recently been shown to bypass audio CAPTCHAs designed around limitations of automatic speech recognition. To address this threat, we introduce reverse-capability verification, a general CAPTCHA framework that reverses the conventional human-easy, machine-hard relationship with respect to the physical ground truth. It selects challenges in which stable human perceptual limitations cause the human-reference answer to differ from the physical ground truth, while machine analysis tends toward that truth. Because the verifier accepts the human-reference answer, advanced solver capabilities and resulting improved recovery of the physical ground truth may instead strengthen the human-machine separation.

Across seven LALMs and eight attack strategies, the maximum observed machine acceptance rates are 19%, 17%, and 3% for HIPI, YINGCHEN, and their composition, respectively, compared with 45-100% for nine prior schemes. A multidimensional human usability study yields corresponding human acceptance rates of 78%, 90%, and 76%, while maintaining competitive efficiency and satisfaction. Further analysis demonstrates that advanced LALMs tend toward physical ground truth. These results show that reverse-capability verification substantially reduces machine acceptance while retaining high human acceptance through an approach different from conventional CAPTCHAs.

Abstract: Static malware detectors that represent each sample as a bag of extracted features, such as permissions, API calls, or imported functions, are cheap to deploy and remain state-of-the-art under temporal drift and in selective classification. Yet, attackers can evade them by adding benign-looking content that outweighs malicious evidence. Canonically, detectors encode the presence of a few thousand features, selected during training, as sparse multi-hot vectors over a fixed vocabulary. Features that emerge after training remain invisible. We show that linear models and multi-layer perceptrons (MLPs) over this input are sum-pooled set models in disguise. Sum-pooling lets added benign features cancel malicious evidence. Instead, we cast malware detection with set-shaped static features as a multiple-instance learning (MIL) problem. To this end, we propose MalSets, which uses max-pooled Deep Sets models with a monotone head to ensure that any malicious evidence remains undiluted; thus, more is never less. Under MIL, a general text encoder maps any feature string to an embedding, thus eliminating the long-standing out-of-vocabulary problem at test time. Max-pooling retains the malicious evidence regardless of how many benign features an attacker adds. Since max-pooling can only grow when features are added, constraining the head alone makes the malware score monotone under additions, even against a white-box attacker adapted to this architecture. While monotone detectors are never evaded by state-of-the-art black-box or white-box attacks, they often significantly pay for this robustness in clean performance since benign features cannot count as evidence. However, by combining a non-monotone set encoder with max-pooling and a monotone head, MalSets maintains the robustness of prior monotone classifiers while achieving state-of-the-art performance.

</details>

Abstract: Consensus lets distributed participants agree on a common state, but it makes monetary validity depend on network connectivity and collective acceptance. We explore making validity intrinsic to a digital coin. We propose an \emph{Intrinsic Value Module (IVM)}: trusted hardware that gives a coin locally verifiable authenticity and spendability, analogous to verifying a signature. Under stated hardware and cryptographic assumptions, the IVM abstraction and our offline transfer protocol move monetary uniqueness from a replicated ledger into non-clonable protected state. The protocol prevents forgery, replay, double credit, and double spending by genuine, uncompromised IVMs; communication failures may lock value but cannot duplicate it. We distinguish local transfer atomicity and asset uniqueness, which the protocol establishes, from global state consistency across disconnected devices, which it does not. The IVM does not eliminate trust; it relocates trust to certified hardware, issuer roots, and non-rollback state. We present the architecture, protocol, and security analysis, and discuss open challenges.

Abstract: Stateful LLM serving engines and agent runtimes reuse execution state and deferred permissions across components and sessions. A cancellation, rollback, or approval is recorded by one component and relied on by another, and recording it does not make the later use valid: a downstream consumer may read state or dispatch an action whose identity, production status, allocation, or target revision has since changed. We call the missing check consumer-time validity and separate two forms. State validity governs what a consumer reads. On vLLM with LMCache, an interrupted KV load leaves unwritten blocks indexed, and a host-only counter rewind desynchronizes the GPU position, so a second tenant generates a schema-valid tool call naming the wrong tenant in 10/10 native-fault runs; the call is recorded and not executed. Authority freshness governs what a consumer executes: on a multi-session approval runtime, an unrevoked deferred grant overwrote a newer revision in one admitted live execution. Output equality cannot test state validity: a fault-free run diverges at token 30 through BF16 batching alone while attended-KV digests match. We therefore specify consumer-bound recovery contracts that, for state recovery, jointly require exclusion, retention, and task completion, and an admissible-witness standard realized as a GPU-free offline trace verifier over scheduler metadata and persisted tool artifacts. On recorded traces the verifier agrees with the reference verdicts in 42/42 frozen GPU-matrix scenarios. An allocation-aware repair satisfies 7/7 triggered vLLM fault predicates on a pinned scheduler-case matrix, where upstream PR 61769 satisfies 2/7, and revision-bound dispatch blocks all 160 deterministic conflicting overwrites in a separate dispatcher cohort. Recovery must be checked where state and authority are consumed, with evidence bound to the consuming request.

</details>

Abstract: Can we tell whether an LLM is watermarked without knowing its secret key? Published black-box tests suggest that carefully designed prompts can reveal the statistical footprint of a watermark. This paper examines what happens when those tests move from controlled reference implementations to a deployed model whose watermarking parameters are undisclosed. We present an audit of the Red-Green watermark presence test on Claude. Across ten initial configurations, the published test never rejects on a model the provider states is watermarked, despite detecting a local reference at comparable sample sizes. Controlled experiments expose a failure mode: including the prompt in the watermark's repeated-context history suppresses marking exactly where the test measures it. The watermark remains active, but the probe becomes blind. Attempts to repair the probe reveal further confounds. Constructed contexts can trigger native digit-to-word preferences, while comparisons within a single response can measure how the model plans its answer. Tokenization changes which contexts the probe actually controls. These findings show why validation on a surrogate model does not establish validity on the model under investigation. A redesigned probe finds a substantial context-dependent shift on Claude Fable that is absent on two sibling controls. However, the pre-registered decision remains inconclusive because selective refusals violate the protocol, and the effect's watermark origin is unresolved. We show how to design controls, distinguish statistical rejection from evidence of watermarking and interpret a null without treating it as proof of absence. We provide researchers and practitioners working on LLM evaluation, watermarking, and provenance with an audit checklist grounded in concrete successes and failures. Code, prompts, and parsed responses are available for reproducing the analyses.

Abstract: Confidential GPUs offer near-native on-device compute performance, but transfers between CPU and GPU TEEs through untrusted host memory require bulk CPU encryption and decryption, even for unchanged data. Existing software techniques overlap or parallelize these operations but retain their per-byte CPU cost. Reusing exported ciphertext could avoid this work, but GPU TEE firmware enforces direction-specific encryption keys and fresh per-transfer initialization vectors (IVs), preventing direct reuse.
  We introduce ciphertext rebinding, a purely software primitive enabling repeated and cross-GPU ciphertext reuse on existing GPU TEEs without hardware changes, while preserving confidentiality, integrity, and freshness. Specifically, ciphertext rebinding exploits the separation between ciphertext-dependent and IV-dependent components in GPU TEE's AES-GCM authentication: a GPU-precomputed summary under the destination key can enable the CPU to update authentication tags without processing the payload. With this modification, GPU's subsequent read on the retained ciphertext can still proceed through the GPU's hardware-fixed copy-engine interfaces, which cannot be modified by software. An additional GPU correction step can then compensate for differences in the keys and IVs to recover the original plaintext. By retaining GPU-exported ciphertext in untrusted host memory, ciphertext rebinding avoids bulk CPU decryption and re-encryption, improving effective bandwidth for GPU-state restoration and sharing. We implement this primitive in HiPerfCC-StateReuse through only driver and library changes, combining batched submissions with pipelined GPU processing and copy-engine transfers. We evaluate transfer efficiency and CPU overhead through microbenchmarks, and application performance through three case studies: model switching, key-value cache offloading, and cross-instance cache sharing.

</details>

Abstract: Safety alignment is widely used to align large language models (LLMs) to produce helpful and harmless responses. However, an attacker can plant a jailbreak backdoor in an LLM such that adding a universal trigger word or phrase to a harmful prompt elicits attacker-desired responses. Existing jailbreak backdoor attacks induce distinct behaviors on backdoored inputs and safety samples (harmful prompts paired with refusal responses), making the backdoors susceptible to subsequent safety alignment and advanced defenses. In this paper, we propose a persistent jailbreak backdoor attack that reduces the divergence between clean and trigger-conditioned continuations during LLM decoding, making backdoored samples difficult to distinguish from safety samples. We also introduce a deferred-response mechanism that postpones harmful generations, further disguising the backdoor behavior. We evaluate our attack, PJBack, on eight LLMs from five model families, ranging from 7B to 70B parameters. PJBack achieves an average attack success rate of 93.9% and bypasses all seven evaluated defenses while preserving benign utility. In contrast, five existing backdoor attacks either achieve limited attack success or fail to withstand the evaluated defenses.

</details>

Abstract: Zero-knowledge virtual machines (zkVMs) prove correct execution of programs and underpin systems securing billions of dollars in digital assets. Their security depends on alignment between the instruction set architecture (ISA) that programs target, such as RISC-V, and the constraint system, polynomial equations that check the prover's record of every intermediate value (the witness). Mismatches, or semantic gaps, come in two kinds: underconstraint bugs let a malicious prover forge a proof of an execution the ISA forbids (a soundness violation), and divergence bugs make the zkVM itself deviate from the ISA or crash, so an honest prover cannot prove a legitimate execution (a completeness violation). Both cluster at corner cases such as division by zero that conventional fuzzing almost never reaches, and underconstraint bugs leave program output unchanged. Our key insight is that these corner cases correspond to constraint obligations derived from the ISA specification and the proof system's algebraic structure. We build Beak, a reinforcement-learning fuzzer in which obligations replace three random decisions: they supply inputs that trigger each obligation, reward executions that trigger new combinations of obligations, and direct witness tampering to the step where an obligation is triggered. Beak flags divergence bugs by comparing registers against a reference RISC-V emulator, and underconstraint bugs by checking whether the constraints accept a tampered witness. On six leading production RISC-V zkVMs, Beak detects 25 of 37 known bugs (67.6%) versus 5 (13.5%) for the prior state of the art, and discovers 14 previously unknown bugs, all responsibly disclosed and patched.

</details>

Abstract: Complex packing in CKKS stores two real streams in the real and imaginary lanes of each slot. Direct squaring couples the lanes, while squaring them separately requires two ciphertext-ciphertext multiplications (HMults). DuetFHE is a GPU compiler that uses a phase transformation to square both lanes independently with one HMult. It reuses dual-lane squares in polynomial activations and the modular-reduction stage of CKKS bootstrapping. The compiler further plans ciphertext scales and reuses conjugates to sustain the complex-packed representation across stages. On a single RTX 5090, DuetFHE accelerates four polynomial activations by $1.60$--$1.79\times$ over direct evaluation on two real ciphertexts. For two-image AESPA ResNet-20 inference, it maintains packed execution across convolutions, square activations, and bootstrapping, achieving a $1.97\times$ end-to-end speedup over the fastest tested EasyFHE batched baseline on both the RTX 5090 and RTX PRO 6000.

Abstract: Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) enables computation directly on encrypted data, but its substantial computational cost hinders adoption in privacy-preserving artificial intelligence (AI) applications. Neural processing units (NPUs) already deployed in AI infrastructure provide a practical platform for FHE acceleration. However, after mapping the number theoretic transform (NTT) to matrix multiplications on Ascend NPUs, modular arithmetic and representation conversion on the Vector backend still incur substantial overhead at stage boundaries. Evaluation-key material of key switching also increases storage and data-transfer costs, and whole-limb task assignment causes tail load imbalance.
  We present NPUFHE, an end-to-end CKKS execution system optimized for Ascend NPUs. To reduce modular recovery and twiddle costs in NTT, we propose Boundary-Optimized NTT, which eliminates intra-axis modular recovery, compiles intra-axis twiddle factors into matrix constants, and introduces two-level folded modular recovery at axis endpoints. To alleviate evaluation-key storage and transfer pressure, we design Compact Shoup encoding, which halves the key representation through local coefficient recovery. Finally, we propose Cost-Aware Parallel Tiling to mitigate tail load imbalance caused by whole-limb task assignment. It uses a cost model to adaptively partition tail tasks and smooth stepwise latency increases.
  We integrate the above designs into OpenFHE. On one Ascend 910B4, full-packing CKKS bootstrapping with real evaluation keys takes 0.95 s, encrypted CIFAR-10 ResNet-20 inference with bootstrapping requires 9.87 s per image, and encrypted MNIST inference achieves an amortized latency of 19.81 ms per image. The peak NTT throughput of NPUFHE reaches up to $184.22\times$, $2.04\times$, and $4.46\times$ that of OpenFHE on CPU, TensorFHE, and CROSS, respectively.

Abstract: LLM coding agents authorize shell commands by parsing a command string against a policy and passing the raw string to a system shell, so the gate adjudicates one reading while the shell executes another. We present ParseGap, a differential testing framework that runs each command through an unmodified vendor checker and GNU Bash 5.2 in an isolated container, flagging a violation whenever an approved command produces policy-violating runtime effects. Across eight production coding agents, ParseGap identifies 17 root causes across three divergence boundaries: at the parse, where an upstream tree-sitter-bash defect hides a second command from Mistral Vibe and Gemini CLI; at the utility, where every checker permits read-only binaries whose arguments write files or launch programs (9-47 of 55 cases); and at confinement, where an allow rule in OpenAI Codex CLI disables its read-only OS sandbox. Live trials across two frontier models triggered policy violations in all 16 agent-model pairs (14 under configured restrictions). To eliminate the second reading, we design ShellPlan, which compiles a strict Bash subset once into a typed execution plan, validates utility arguments against positive capability specifications, and dispatches approved plans directly via execve without a shell. Over a formal operational model, we prove single-interpretation dispatch and bound touched programs and files under audited assumptions. Abstract: Backdoor attacks pose a significant security threat to deep neural networks (DNNs) deployed on AI inference engines and hardware accelerators. By embedding hidden triggers during training, these attacks induce malicious behavior while maintaining high accuracy on benign inputs. Multi-trigger backdoor attacks are particularly challenging to detect and mitigate because of the distributed and stealthy nature of their effects. To address this challenge, this paper proposes a training-free Spectral Subspace Filtering framework that detects and suppresses backdoor-induced activations by analyzing latent feature representations. Exploiting the geometric separation between benign and malicious feature distributions, the framework employs Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) to identify the dominant malicious subspace and performs inference-time spectral scrubbing through activation projection. This approach suppresses backdoor effects without model retraining, requiring only a small set of triggered samples collected offline. Experimental evaluation across CNN and transformer architectures, including AlexNet, ResNet18, VGG16, MobileNetV2, and Vision Transformer (ViT), demonstrates that the proposed method reduces the attack success rate (ASR) from 94.2-99.8% to an average of 2.07%, while preserving clean accuracy of up to 98.54% with low inference overhead. The framework also demonstrates robustness across varying poisoning rates and multiple concurrent triggers, highlighting its potential as a lightweight runtime defense for enhancing the security and reliability of AI inference engines against backdoor attacks.

Abstract: The evolving cellular networks continue to rely on the legacy GPRS Tunneling Protocol-User Plane (GTP-U) to encapsulate and route user data traffic across the data plane. Experimental results indicate that the XDP-DDM detects and drops malicious nested GTP-U packets at line rate, while the UP-TA and CP-MO asynchronously report and revoke the attacker's network access, respectively. Under a 10,000 packets-per-second cross-slice nested GTP-U flood, the proposed solution preserves the baseline performance of the victim slices while introducing minimal overhead. We also show that the proposed solution has minimal impact on the user's throughput and round-trip time.

Abstract: Relative security (RS) judges a system against a reference system, but has only been applied to confidentiality. We introduce relative integrity (RI), which states that every behaviour a system exhibits under an attacker's influence must be one the reference can exhibit for the same trusted inputs. RS and RI each choose their own reference runs, so together they accept attacks whose leak and effects the reference reproduces only in different runs. We therefore develop a further property, relative robustness (RR), which asks the same runs to reproduce both and is strictly stronger than RS and RI together.
  We give proof methods for RI and RR and a disproof method for RI, and apply them in two domains: (1) LLM agents under prompt injection, where the reference runs the same plan with a model that never obeys untrusted content, and (2) C programs partitioned across an SGX or TrustZone enclave boundary, where the reference keeps every function inside. We evaluate the properties on models of five published attacks, a support desk, an OP-TEE HOTP token and common agent defences. Two cases pass RS and RI but fail RR: an injection that makes the support desk's model accept any customer, as one input did in the Tensor Trust game, and the simulation step of the attack on Secret Network, in which an SGX enclave reveals a transfer's outcome without committing it. Our work is fully mechanised in Isabelle/HOL, with the enclave programs verified in C using AutoCorres2.

</details>

Abstract: Commercial AI agents increasingly incorporate long-term memory (both persistent and chat-history) to enable more accurate and personalized responses, raises significant privacy and policy concerns. Yet little is known about user privacy risks associated with memory behavior, in particular, about memory storage, disclosure, transparency and deletion control. To bridge this gap, we propose FORGETMENOT, an auditing framework for analyzing the privacy risks of long term memory in commercial AI agents. Using our framework, we collect around 3800 multi-turn sessions comprising around 6M tokens across five popular AI agents. Our results show that agents store a wide range of personal attributes, including demographics, health, and sensitive details. Furthermore, user visible memory reveals substantially less (around 20% on average) attributes than the agents store in practice, exposing a significant transparency gap. Our stealth memory injection (SMI) technique further increases the amount of personal and sensitive attribute stored in memory, raising it by 25% and 15% for Copilot and Claude, respectively. We further observed memory deletion control is unreliable, as all five agents exhibit non-zero post deletion leakage, including health and self-harm related attributes. Finally, we demonstrate the feasibility of memory disclosure to a 3rd-party email recipient as a proof-of-concept, as Gemini shows 31.9% leakage rate. Together, our findings reveal notable privacy risks in commercial agents memory. Finally, we discuss both design implications and regulatory recommendations to protect users memory privacy in commercial agents.

</details>

Abstract: Machine learning-based intrusion detection systems can improve IoT security, but high-dimensional traffic features and imbalanced datasets may reduce model reliability and deployment feasibility in resource-constrained environments. This study presents a lightweight binary IoT intrusion detection framework using an approximately balanced CICIoT2023 derived dataset, feature reduction, and comparative machine learning evaluation. The original multi-class attack labels were converted into two classes: Benign and Malicious. To reduce class bias, malicious traffic was randomly undersampled to obtain an approximately balanced binary dataset. Information Gain Ratio and Gini Decrease were then used to rank traffic features and generate reduced feature subsets of 38, 30, 20, 10, 5, and 3 features. Five machine learning classifiers, including Random Forest(RF), Decision Tree(DT), Naive Bayes(NB), Logistic Regression(LR), and Support Vector Machine(SVM), were evaluated using 5-fold cross-validation. The results show that Random Forest achieved the strongest overall performance. In particular,the 20-feature subset achieved accuracy of 99.1% and MCC of 0.982, matching the full-feature model while reducing feature dimensionality by approximately 47.4%. The 10-feature subset also maintained strong performance with accuracy of 98.9% and MCC of 0.979, reducing the feature space by approximately 73.7%. These findings demonstrate that balanced binary classification and progressive feature reduction can preserve detection effectiveness while improving lightweight IDS feasibility.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) evolve from text generation to autonomous software-engineering execution, automated vulnerability exploitability verification has emerged as a vital frontier in software quality assurance and security validation. Existing security benchmarks, however, largely judge models by binary pass/fail outcomes or coarse stage milestones. Such outcome-centric scoring obscures fine-grained failure dynamics across long-horizon execution and conflates the intrinsic capability of a base LLM with external agent scaffolding. We present POMA (Pwn-Oriented Model Assessment), a process-level evaluation methodology and platform that isolates base LLMs under a unified execution baseline -- fixing task inputs, tool interfaces, target environments, feedback formats, and execution budgets -- while measuring intermediate states and transitions from runtime evidence. Evaluating four frontier base LLMs across 55 CTF Pwn challenges and 10 real-world vulnerability reproduction tasks exposes persistent capability attrition along the exploitation chain: from vulnerability knowledge to executable implementation, from client-side actions to verified target states, from failure localization to precise repair, and across iterative revisions. In real-world software, models further struggle to satisfy format, semantic, differential, and crash-identity constraints simultaneously. By pinpointing where execution chains break down, POMA provides an empirical foundation for trustworthy capability assessment and principled vulnerability-agent design. Our benchmark catalogs, process-measurement evaluator, and sample harness are publicly available at https://github.com/maple-pwn/poma-release .

</details>

Abstract: System provenance graphs help reconstruct attacks through causal dependencies, but these connections alone do not explain how events accomplish an adversary's goal. Cyber threat intelligence (CTI) provides the necessary security knowledge, yet linking it to execution evidence requires bridging differences in meaning and granularity between events and attack relations amid scarce labels and sparse relevant evidence. We present LODESTONE, a cross-graph alignment system that links observed system events to CTI attack relations to support evidence-grounded attack reports. LODESTONE uses a novel CTI-driven training-data engine and an expert-verified benchmark of edge-level correspondences and reference attack reports. The engine pairs real CTI knowledge graphs with provenance graphs from isolated executions. A large language model (LLM) agent recursively improves correspondences by checking execution evidence and repairing retrieval, parsing, and reproduction failures; experts independently verify benchmark correspondences. LODESTONE combines text cross-encoder and directed-graph relevance scores to extract compact alert-centered subgraphs, retrieves article-level knowledge graphs through node- and edge-level dual-softmax assignment, and uses a fine-tuned 4B-parameter LLM to align event combinations with CTI attack steps. We evaluate LODESTONE on 27 expert-verified attack cases amid continuous benign activity from real applications, using 713 CTI articles. LLM judgments aligned with multiple human experts yield an attack-step description score of 82%, and attack identification reaches 91%, versus 73% and 32% using the same generator with provenance alone. Baseline pipelines and coding agents achieve description scores of at most 57%. Human-verified correspondences further demonstrate advantages in balancing attack-edge coverage with subgraph size, CTI retrieval, and cross-graph alignment.

</details>

Abstract: Software engineering (SE) agents can complete diverse engineering tasks by interacting with the development environment via tools that access files, run commands, and download resources. Unfortunately, the same ability that makes SE agents useful also makes them prone to performing unintended malicious actions through prompt injections, malicious tools, or supply-chain attacks. Existing defenses that analyze the agent trajectory may fail to detect such attacks, as they inspect only the initial prompt and the sequence of tool calls, but not the actual actions that are executed in the environment. This paper presents AgentBouncer, a novel runtime enforcement system that prevents SE agents from performing malicious actions by mediating, and if necessary denying, system calls that agents trigger via tool calls. The core of AgentBouncer is an LLM arbiter that judges system calls along two axes, necessity and safety for the current task. It denies unsafe system calls, and the less necessary a system call appears, the more certain the arbiter has to be about its safety to allow it. To manage runtime overhead, preseeded policy rules and hierarchy-aware caching ensure that the LLM arbiter is only invoked for key decisions. We evaluate AgentBouncer on a dataset of 340 attacks on the development environment, covering four attack vectors, combined with benign issue resolution tasks from SWE-bench Verified. AgentBouncer reduces the attack success rate from 99.41% for an unsupervised agent to 10.59%, which is 18.23-73.53 percentage points lower than competitive state-of-the-art defenses. At the same time, AgentBouncer preserves the task success rate of the agent, with only 0.25% failed benign tool calls. Per benign task, AgentBouncer adds 47 seconds and USD 0.0036 in LLM costs to an unsupervised agent, whereas the closest existing runtime defense adds 4.5x as much time and 9.6x as much cost.

</details>

Abstract: LLM agents act through tool calls: the model writes the arguments of a call as JSON, a guard decides whether the call may run, and an executor runs it. The same bytes are parsed several times, by the provider, gateways, SDKs, frameworks, the guard, and the executor. JSON leaves some choices to each parser, such as which copy of a repeated key counts. When the guard and the executor choose differently, the guard approves one call, the executor runs another, and the audit log records the approved one. We introduce this attack, tool-call smuggling, and study it across the stack. We describe six ways in which the same bytes yield different calls and build a differential testing harness that gives identical bytes to 17 pinned parser and validator configurations; most pairs disagree on some inputs, and even two official MCP SDKs accept different inputs. On tasks from $τ$-bench and AgentDojo, an attacker who can rewrite the bytes in transit cancels another customer's reservation and creates a file that the guard blocks by name, while the audit log names only the approved call. Content the model reads splits guard and executor only inside a string: ten models we test write no repeated or disguised key into the call itself where we see it, but copy one verbatim into JSON text in an argument, which a Python guard and a Go executor read differently. A malicious server copying a legitimate tool's public schema and adding one field, which a note explains, gets the model to fill it, unseen by the guard, with user data. Parsing each call once, JSON text included, keeping only the fields of the approved schema, and handing it to the executor, sealed with its call ID in transit, stops all 7,412 crafted inputs and every attack we built.

</details>

Abstract: Anomaly-based malicious traffic detection learns decision boundaries for normal behavior and identifies deviations as anomalies. However, normal behavior varies across networks and changes dynamically, limiting adaptability and increasing retraining costs. Despite this variability, our measurements show that clients accessing the same service exhibit consistent flow structures and interaction patterns, since each service provides specific functionality. We therefore construct service-specific decision boundaries. However, variations in personalized requests make it difficult to construct stable boundaries.
  To address this challenge, we propose IDSMorph, a self-adaptive malicious traffic detection system that learns consistency-guided embeddings through two key designs: (1) a flow structure consistency builder, which constructs contrastive samples capturing consistent embeddings within services; and (2) a service interaction consistency builder, which captures local structural consistency among different clients and global behavioral consistency among clients accessing that service. IDSMorph constructs dynamic decision boundaries from the majority of similar embeddings within each service and dynamically updates them through a differencing algorithm that mitigates the transient traffic shifts within short observation windows. Experiments show that IDSMorph's detection F1 score decreases by no more than 0.12 points across network environments, compared with an average decrease of 55.43 points for prior methods. In real-world deployment with red-team operators independently choosing attack strategies and timing, IDSMorph achieves an F1 score 46.81 points higher than other methods.

</details>

Abstract: Agent skills are reusable packages that extend a large language model (LLM) agent with scripts, third-party dependencies, and natural-language instructions. An agent builds a skill's arguments from user requests and untrusted external content, so a flaw left by a benign author can escalate a prompt injection to code execution. Existing studies target malicious skills or a single attack vector, and none tests whether an agent can reach such flaws. We present the first registry-scale empirical study of the supply chain attack surface arising from unintentional vulnerabilities in agent skills, over all 48,682 skills in ClawHub. The study covers two carried-over risks, dependencies and first-party code, and two agent-native ones, install instructions and credentials printed to stdout. We screen all skills by static analysis, manually review all 3,428 cases flagged by the four main detectors, and test reachability by running sampled flagged skills in a sandbox and with a live LLM agent under indirect prompt injection. First, dependency risk carries over in a different form: 49.6% of the skills with resolvable dependencies depend on a version with a known CVE, and unlike npm packages, which mostly become vulnerable after release, 78.3% of them are born vulnerable, already exposed at publication. Second, few of these CVEs are reachable: agent input reaches the vulnerable function in only 3.0% of the affected skills, whereas 21 of 22 sampled first-party flaws are exploited end to end in the sandbox. Third, a live agent executes the injected payload in 48.5% of the sampled flagged skills, but almost always in its own shell before the skill runs, and the payload reaches a flaw in the skill itself in only 5.0%. Hence, registries should prioritize first-party flaws over dependency alerts, and agent runtimes should not pass skill arguments through a shell.

</details>

Abstract: Harnesses secure Computer-Using Agents by enforcing security constraints and defending against prompt injection attacks. However, we find that two core security mechanisms of agent harnesses remain vulnerable. First, consistency checking prevents agents from deviating from legitimate user objectives, while attackers can bypass it by embedding malicious actions that appear logically consistent with legitimate task execution. Second, risk auditing proactively examines high-risk operations. Yet, the auditing process itself would introduce additional exploitable actions that violate security boundaries. To evaluate above defects we found, we propose Conartist, a cascaded adaptive prompt injection framework comprising two progressive stages. First, Conartist employs a goal-pollution strategy to establish deceptive causal dependencies between legitimate user objectives and malicious operations, progressively embedding attack steps into legitimate task workflows. However, these task-coupled attacks remain susceptible to risk auditing. To address this limitation, Conartist further analyzes security-auditing feedback from controlled attack exploration to identify exploitable auditing patterns. Conartist then adaptively refines its deception strategies to either avoid triggering risk auditing or induce additional high-risk operations. Extensive experiments across five backbone models demonstrate the effectiveness of Conartist, achieving attack success rates of 32.12% on 576-instance tool use benchmark and 66.67% in the Reddit GUI benchmark, outperforming the SOTA attack by 14.41 and 29.17, respectively. By analyzing one of the most popular Agent, Openclaw, we furthermore concluded seven realistic exploitable attack patterns, which reveals that security mechanisms designed to prevent malicious actions can themselves be manipulated to facilitate attacks.

</details>

Abstract: Frontier AI governance agreements require compute verification techniques that allow an external observer to confirm that a hardware operator does not run prohibited workloads. Off-chip, analogue hardware signals, such as power draw, have been proposed as a potential information channel for compute verification. Such signals can be measured externally, without the operator's cooperation. We show that the supply current drawn by a GPU can be used to verify the work declared by the GPU operator, without requiring examples of prohibited workloads or evasion strategies. We record 23.4 h of GPU current with a Rogowski coil under idle, training and inference workloads. We consider five evasion strategies an operator might use to disguise prohibited training. We show that a one-class model fitted to the current draw of permitted, declared work (idle and inference) flags at least 99.9% of windows of prohibited training, including training hidden by the five evasion strategies. As a continuous monitor across successive windows, it detects all 188 episodes of prohibited training hidden by time-sharing with declared inference, with a median delay of 3.0 s, and raises one alarm in 8.8 h of declared work. Incomplete or corrupt recordings of the declared work can undermine verification. An incomplete recording fails loudly; with idle missing, the monitor is in alarm for 99.97% of declared idle. A corrupt recording can fail silently; if the operator runs training during the recording, the monitor can miss hidden training while raising no alarm on declared work. These results suggest that operator declarations together with external analogue sensing might offer a practical route to compute verification.

</details>

Abstract: As AI models evolve from text and vision to physical agents, embodied AI faces a fundamentally different attack surface. Prior attacks on embodied systems have used crafted instructions, adversarial visual inputs, and training-time policy modifications; we study environment-only object rearrangements with unchanged models and instructions. We reveal an attack surface in safety-critical spatial relations among existing objects. An adversary who rearranges objects, without modifying instructions or accessing models, can cause hazardous consequences. In this work, we propose EnvTrap, a diagnostic pipeline that constructs paired safe, trap, and null-trap (benign but misleading) embodied scenarios. We demonstrate that environment-only perturbations raise hazardous-action rates to an average of 83.4% across the five evaluated action policies, with similar vulnerability patterns confirmed in world models. On a consequence-prediction task, model accuracy remains near chance, while human evaluators achieve high accuracy. We further propose a consequence-aware defense that reduces trap trigger rates by an average of 76.3 percentage points across action policies in simulation and by 62.9 percentage points pooled across physical backends. Together, these findings link environment-only attacks to a broader deficit in physical reasoning: embodied models recognize task-relevant objects yet fail to anticipate how spatial relations change the consequences of acting on them. EnvTrap makes this gap directly testable and turns hazardous layouts into training scenarios for safer embodied action. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/INKSIR/Envtrap.

</details>

Abstract: We present $\mathsf{LaTSEC}$, a three-round lattice-based threshold signature scheme with native non-interactive identifiable abort. Each party holds one small-norm signing share and is associated with one public abort key, against which its partial signature is verified directly, without zero-knowledge proofs or an additional interactive protocol. Prior lattice-based threshold signatures that identify misbehaving parties rely on one of three mechanisms: zero-knowledge proofs, an additional interactive protocol, or an everywhere-short secret sharing scheme in which each party holds multiple signing shares, each with its own public abort key. Compared with schemes that use the third mechanism, $\mathsf{LaTSEC}$ substantially reduces per-party storage. The construction uses $\mathsf{SEC}$, a recent ramp everywhere-short secret sharing scheme based on lossless expanders that gives each party a single short share. We prove correctness, identifiable-abort security, and threshold unforgeability under the standard $\mathsf{MLWE}$ and $\mathsf{MSIS}$ assumptions. The unforgeability proof introduces the Repeated-$\boldsymbol{A}$ Matrix Hint-MLWE problem, a new variant of $\mathsf{MLWE}$ with hints that captures the complete tuple of correlated public abort keys. We rigorously prove that this new variant admits a reduction from the standard $\mathsf{MLWE}$ problem. For 60 parties at 128-bit security, $\mathsf{LaTSEC}$ tolerates up to 6 corrupted parties, and, empirically, a randomly chosen set of at least 46 parties can sign with probability at least 0.99. Each party holds one signing share of about 14.3 KiB and is associated with one 24-KiB public abort key.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in text-to-speech (TTS), voice conversion (VC), and AI-driven speech synthesis enable adversaries to convincingly impersonate trusted individuals during phone calls. Existing defenses often rely on offline analysis, degrade under telephony distortions, or remain vulnerable to adaptive attacks. To address these limitations, we present Ultrasound Watermark, a real-time, smartphone-native framework that protects live calls by binding speech to the caller's physical articulatory motion through acoustic watermarking. During a call, the smartphone emits ultrasonic signals and captures reflections from facial articulatory movements using only its built-in speaker and microphone. Ultrasound Watermark extracts motion features from these reflections and fuses them with outgoing speech to generate a dynamic watermark. Because the ultrasonic measurements are physically and temporally coupled with speech production, they are difficult to spoof without the underlying articulatory motion. A synchronized generator-detector architecture enforces cross-modal speech-motion consistency against replay, synthesized speech, and articulation-mimicking attacks. Ultrasound Watermark operates continuously with less than 200 ms latency overhead and requires no additional hardware. We implement Ultrasound Watermark on iOS and Android and evaluate it using over 2.5 hours of synchronized speech-ultrasound recordings from 22 speakers across eight smartphones and four real-world environments. Abstract: Security benchmarks for LLM agents record whether each attack succeeded and compare agents, attacks, and defenses by attack success rate (ASR). Such comparisons are valid only if the agent, the attack, and the defense cause each verdict. We show that verdicts also depend on content-free state: details of a run that carry no information about the system under test, such as the Python hash seed, the order of tools or evidence, and the scoring of failed runs. We require that this state influence no verdict (decision noninterference) and treat it as untrusted input, because evaluators and requesters of LLM-based gates can choose it. Our framework, TARE (Testing Attribution of Reported Effects), attributes a reported effect to the agent, attack, or defense only if it exceeds the measured influence of content-free state. On Agent Security Bench (ASB), runs with a fixed hash seed are identical for seven agents from four model families, yet changing the seed changes up to 17% of an agent's verdicts while the ASR moves by at most 5 points. The seed acts through the tool order: sorting the tool list instead of reverse-sorting it raises the ASR by up to 19 points, because ASB's naming convention places attacker tools first. When the same evidence is reordered, six of seven LLM judges change their decision on 17-91% of 98 proposed actions. Across eighteen agent-defense combinations, TARE's worst-case test attributes effects to two of three defenses, one of them in opposite directions on two agents, and a test fixed in advance confirms a 4.8-point decrease from the third; in the attributed decreases, 35-69% of the stopped attacks end without any tool call, which ASB counts as a failed attack. Reproducible verdicts are thus not necessarily attributable, and security evaluations of LLM agents should report the influence of content-free state. We release TARE, the ASB patch, and all verdict tables.

</details>

Abstract: Mirror-based LiDAR attacks passively redirect automotive LiDAR's beams to create phantom objects in the vehicle perception. Recent work has demonstrated that these attacks may cause vehicles to brake for a phantom where nothing stands. Every phantom return is a genuine time-of-flight measurement, so current spoofing defenses are not able to differentiate phantoms from real objects. We present MirrorGuard, a physics-informed defense that identifies and removes mirror-induced phantom returns before they affect downstream perception. MirrorGuard tests three consequences of the reflected path. Every phantom bearing crosses the mirror's finite aperture, its opaque surface blocks any direct return beyond it, and the two reflections off the mirror dim the phantom. MirrorGuard identifies a phantom by these cues over multiple scans and removes the reflected beams before it reaches any detector or occupancy grid. On real-world recordings of this attack, MirrorGuard removes 96.31% of the phantom's returns at 98.30% precision, with 99.99% of non-phantom returns untouched. In a closed loop with the CARLA driving simulator and the open-source stack (Autoware), a vehicle without the defense halts for an obstacle that is not present and a following car crashes into its rear. With MirrorGuard, no vehicle produces a halt or a crash. MirrorGuard runs at 59 ms mean per scan on a CPU and is a software-only solution that does not require new hardware or environmental training.

</details>

Abstract: Differentially private zeroth-order optimization (DPZO) is a promising approach for privacy-preserving LLM fine-tuning, offering exceptional memory efficiency. However, its utility is highly sensitive to the clipping threshold $C$, a key DP-specific hyperparameter that controls the trade-off between noise injection and clipping bias. Existing DPZO methods typically tune $C$ by repeatedly evaluating candidate values and reporting the best result, which is computationally expensive and incurs additional privacy cost that must be accounted for under DP. Designing a practical DP procedure for selecting $C$ in DPZO is challenging because no fixed choice performs well across settings, straightforward search-based strategies suffer from poor utility and efficiency, and existing clipping-selection methods for DPSGD do not transfer well to DPZO due to its fundamentally different error regime. In this paper, we propose AutoClip-DPZO, a practical DP method that automatically and efficiently selects $C$ for DPZO. Our key idea is to avoid fine-tuning-based search by reducing clipping selection to a private quantile estimation problem, where the target quantile depends only on public privacy and training parameters. To derive this selection rule, we develop a novel characterization of DPZO error as a function of $C$ that captures both noise variance and clipping bias while remaining accurate and easy to optimize. Experiments show that AutoClip-DPZO achieves utility comparable to that obtained with the optimal $C$, while avoiding repeated fine-tuning-based search and incurring negligible additional overhead. Moreover, AutoClip-DPZO does not rely on sensitive hyperparameters.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly use third-party tools to complete user tasks. Even without malicious intent, they may fill broad or weakly constrained fields with private information that is unnecessary or inappropriate for the receiving tool. Such calls can satisfy the tool schema and complete the task, yet still violate minimum-necessary disclosure. We introduce ArguShield, a mediation layer that intercepts proposed tool calls before execution. It evaluates each candidate value using three signals: Value Provenance, Task Relevance, and Disclosure Boundary. ArguShield releases justified values, defers values that need user confirmation, and withholds those outside the permitted scope. We evaluate ArguShield with four tool-calling models on ArgPrivBench, a benchmark of 120 tasks across Travel, Shopping, and Healthcare. Unprotected agents frequently over-disclose private information despite high task success. Prompt-only protection reduces unauthorized disclosure but substantially lowers task success. ArguShield reduces unauthorized silent release to 9% on Standard tasks and 6% on High-risk tasks while preserving recoverable task success under simulated user confirmation.

Abstract: Tool-augmented Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to resource-exhaustion attacks that amplify model-token consumption through persistent context amplification, large tool output amplification, and error-induced agentic loops. Despite growing attention to agent security and resource-abuse risks, a systematic defense framework specifically targeting these attacks remains lacking. Meanwhile, existing general-purpose agent defense frameworks, which address a broader range of security threats, often rely on prompt-level safeguards or coarse-grained resource limits and lack deterministic runtime mechanisms for controlling token consumption. They also provide limited support for balancing fault recovery against the risk of repeated resource-consuming execution. To address these limitations, we propose a trust-anchored closed-loop defense framework consisting of four phases: certificate-guided orchestration with deterministic handoffs, calibrated one-time node budgeting, Gateway-based runtime enforcement, and bounded recovery through a shared, non-resettable recovery pool. By accounting for both input and output tokens across all model invocations, our experiments demonstrate that the framework bounds cumulative token consumption, mitigates resource-amplification attacks, and preserves benign task utility under trusted execution assumptions.

The processor-side cost of a signature falls from over a second under per-kernel control to 0.53 ms, fixed whatever the number of rejections, and the fabric's time per attempt varies by less than 0.1% under a saturating workload. Under NASA JPL's F Prime with attitude-control and imaging loads, two nodes with fabric-generated, ground-certified keys exchanged 3,575 signed frames, every frame was accepted, and no attitude deadline was missed. A cycle profile places the remaining distance to dedicated cores in two serial units.
